=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are given an array nums.
A split of an array nums is beautiful if:

The array nums is split into three subarrays: nums1, nums2, and nums3, such that nums can be formed by concatenating nums1, nums2, and nums3 in that order.
The subarray nums1 is a prefix of nums2 OR nums2 is a prefix of nums3.

Return the number of ways you can make this split.
 
Example 1:

Input: nums = [1,1,2,1]
Output: 2
Explanation:
The beautiful splits are:

A split with nums1 = [1], nums2 = [1,2], nums3 = [1].
A split with nums1 = [1], nums2 = [1], nums3 = [2,1].


Example 2:

Input: nums = [1,2,3,4]
Output: 0
Explanation:
There are 0 beautiful splits.

 
Constraints:

1 <= nums.length <= 5000
0 <= nums[i] <= 50

Use the following starter code to write the final solution.
```python
class Solution:
    def beautifulSplits(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        
```

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:

class Solution:
    def beautifulSplits(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        ans = 0
        max_d = (n - 1) // 2  # because i>=1 for B and 2d <= n-1; A also 2d <= n-1
        for d in range(1, max_d + 1):
            # compute lcp for pairs (i, i+d) from i = n-d-1 down to 0
            next_lcp = 0
            # We'll iterate i from n-d-1 down to 0
            # But count B only for i >=1 and i+2d <= n.
            # We can compute all i; for i maybe > n-2d no B.
            for i in range(n - d - 1, -1, -1):
                if nums[i] == nums[i + d]:
                    cur = next_lcp + 1
                else:
                    cur = 0
                # A: if i == 0 and 2*d <= n-1 and cur >= d: ans += n - 2*d
                # Since d <= max_d, 2d <= n-1, so yes. But need ensure j from 2d to n-1 count n - 2d. Wait if 2d <= n-1. For d=max_d, okay. If n=5,d=2, 2d=4<=4, count n-2d=1. A for i=2? nums1 length2, nums2 start at 2, need j>=4, j<=4, count1. Good.
                if i == 0 and cur >= d:
                    ans += n - 2 * d
                # B: if i >= 1 and i + 2*d <= n and cur >= d:
                if i >= 1 and i + 2 * d <= n and cur >= d:
                    ans += 1
                next_lcp = cur
        return ans

Let's verify Z algorithm implementation:
def z_algorithm(s):
    n = len(s)
    z = [0]*n
    l = r = 0
    for i in range(1,n):
        if i <= r:
            z[i] = min(r-i+1, z[i-l])
        while i+z[i] < n and s[z[i]] == s[i+z[i]]:
            z[i] += 1
        if i+z[i]-1 > r:
            l, r = i, i+z[i]-1
    return z
Need values nums list, compare ints. Good.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def beautifulSplits(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        if n < 3:
            return 0

        # Z-function: z[i] = LCP(nums, nums[i:])
        z = [0] * n
        l = r = 0
        for i in range(1, n):
            if i <= r:
                z[i] = min(r - i + 1, z[i - l])
            while i + z[i] < n and nums[z[i]] == nums[i + z[i]]:
                z[i] += 1
            if i + z[i] - 1 > r:
                l = r = i? Wait set l=i, r=i+z[i]-1. Need code:
                l = i
                r = i + z[i] - 1
        max_d = (n - 1) // 2

        # Count splits where nums1 is prefix of nums2.
        ans = 0
        for i in range(1, max_d + 1):
            if z[i] >= i:
                ans += n - 2 * i

        # Count splits where nums2 is prefix of nums3, and subtract those also counted by first case.
        # For fixed d = len(nums2), check all starts i of nums2.
        for d in range(1, max_d + 1):
            next_lcp = 0
            # i from n-d-1 down to 0; lcp[i] = LCP(nums[i:], nums[i+d:])
            for i in range(n - d - 1, -1, -1):
                if nums[i] == nums[i + d]:
                    cur = next_lcp + 1
                else:
                    cur = 0

                if i >= 1 and i + 2 * d <= n and cur >= d:
                    ans += 1  # B split
                    if d >= i and z[i] >= i:
                        ans -= 1  # it was already counted in A
                next_lcp = cur

        return ans

Wait, we are adding B and subtracting inter inline. This yields ans = A + B - inter. Good. But note if A count includes only i<=max_d. For inter, z[i]>=i and d>=i, with d<=max_d, so i<=max_d. Good.

But is it okay to subtract inter inline after adding B? Yes.

Let's ensure code indentation.

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def beautifulSplits(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        if n < 3:
            return 0

        z = [0] * n
        l = r = 0
        for i in range(1, n):
            if i <= r:
                z[i] = min(r - i + 1, z[i - l])
            while i + z[i] < n and nums[z[i]] == nums[i + z[i]]:
                z[i] += 1
            if i + z[i] - 1 > r:
                l = i
                r = i + z[i] - 1

        max_d = (n - 1) // 2

        ans = 0
        for i in range(1, max_d + 1):
            if z[i] >= i:
                ans += n - 2 * i

        for d in range(1, max_d + 1):
            next_lcp = 0
            for i in range(n - d - 1, 0, -1):
                if nums[i] == nums[i + d]:
                    cur = next_lcp + 1
                else:
                    cur = 0

                if cur >= d:
                    ans += 1
                    if d >= i and z[i] >= i:
                        ans -= 1

                next_lcp = cur

        return ans
```

=== ANSWER ===
is too long. We need final code only. The above reasoning is internal; final should be code block. Ensure no extra text.

Let's finalize with code.</think>```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def beautifulSplits(self, nums: List[int]) -> int:
        n = len(nums)
        if n < 3:
            return 0

        # Z-function: z[i] = LCP(nums, nums[i:])
        z = [0] * n
        l = r = 0
        for i in range(1, n):
            if i <= r:
                z[i] = min(r - i + 1, z[i - l])
            while i + z[i] < n and nums[z[i]] == nums[i + z[i]]:
                z[i] += 1
            if z[i] > 0 and i + z[i] - 1 > r:
                l = i
                r = i + z[i] - 1

        max_d = (n - 1) // 2

        # Count splits where nums1 is a prefix of nums2.
        ans = 0
        for i in range(1, max_d + 1):
            if z[i] >= i:
                ans += n - 2 * i

        # Count splits where nums2 is a prefix of nums3.
        # For fixed d = len(nums2), compute LCP(nums[i:], nums[i+d:]) along a diagonal.
        for d in range(1, max_d + 1):
            next_lcp = 0
            for i in range(n - d - 1, 0, -1):
                if nums[i] == nums[i + d]:
                    cur = next_lcp + 1
                else:
                    cur = 0

                if cur >= d:
                    # If this split was already counted by the first case, skip it.
                    if not (d >= i and z[i] >= i):
                        ans += 1

                next_lcp = cur

        return ans
```
